The [2023 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Formula_One_World_Championship) celebrated the 60th anniversary of the team's founding, with the season's car named the [MCL60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL60) in commemoration.[^cite_note-mcl60-167][^cite_note-mcl601-168] The season started with multiple technical issues,[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] causing them to release a public statement after the [Saudi Arabian Grand Prix](65766701.md), announcing certain organisational changes.[^cite_note-changes-174] After not scoring points in the first two races, Norris and Piastri finished the chaotic [Australian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Australian_Grand_Prix) in sixth and eighth place respectively, with Piastri scoring his first points in Formula One and for McLaren.[^cite_note-175] During the midpoint of the season, McLaren's trajectory began to increase with upgrades being introduced at the [Austrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Austrian_Grand_Prix)[^cite_note-176] and [Singapore Grands Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Singapore_Grand_Prix). The upgrades were an immediate success, and McLaren attained its first podium with Norris since the 2021 Italian Grand Prix. Piastri achieved his first career podium at the [Japanese Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Japanese_Grand_Prix)[^cite_note-177] and won the [Qatar Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Qatar_Grand_Prix) sprint race, marking McLaren's first sprint race victory in Formula One; the main race saw a record be broken for the fastest pit stop in Formula One, setting a pit stop time of 1.80 seconds, 0.02 quicker than the previous record set by Red Bull Racing in the [2019 Brazilian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Brazilian_Grand_Prix).[^cite_note-178] During the [Abu Dhabi Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Abu_Dhabi_Grand_Prix) weekend, McLaren signed an extension to use Mercedes engines until 2030.[^cite_note-\:2-20] McLaren took fourth place in the Constructors' Championship, with Norris taking sixth and Piastri taking ninth in the drivers' championship.&#x20;

#### 2024–2025: World Championship years

Orange McLaren MCL38 at the Dutch Grand Prix

An evolution of the accomplished MCL60, the MCL38 scored consistent results after a mid-season upgrade, propelling them into championship contention and subsequent Constructors' Championship victory.

The success of the MCL60 provided McLaren, who finished the 2023 season in fourth, allowing for more testing time,[^cite_note-179] numerous opportunities to capitalise in performance for the [2024](72504121.md) season. Retaining Norris and Piastri, the team hired [David Sanchez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Sanchez_\(engineer\)) from [Scuderia Ferrari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scuderia_Ferrari) to lead development[^cite_note-180] alongside [Peter Prodromou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Prodromou) and [Neil Houldey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Houldey) (after a restructuring, [Rob Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rob_Marshall_\(motorsport\)) would lead development[^cite_note-181]) for the 2024 car, titled the [MCL38](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL38). At the start of the season, the MCL38 proved to be the third-fastest car overall behind Ferrari's [SF-24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferrari_SF-24). The SF-24 had superior tyre management, but the MCL38 excelled in qualifying.[^cite_note-\:12-182] McLaren revealed a substantial upgrade package that would be utilised for the [Miami Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Miami_Grand_Prix). The upgrade's success would be confirmed after Norris took the lead from Verstappen and went on to win the race, marking his maiden Formula One career victory.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] McLaren expected the upgrades to improve the car's performance across all conditions;[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] the upgrade was much more successful than expected, to the point the team needed to investigate it.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] By the [British Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_British_Grand_Prix), the MCL38 had been established as the fastest car.[^cite_note-190] One of these successes included a 1-2 finish at the [Hungarian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Hungarian_Grand_Prix), McLaren's first since the [2021 Italian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Italian_Grand_Prix). A mid-season upgrade introduced for the [Dutch Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Dutch_Grand_Prix) further improved performance,[^cite_note-191] with Norris winning by over 27 seconds ahead of second-placed Verstappen.[^cite_note-192] Piastri's triumph at the [Azerbaijan Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Azerbaijan_Grand_Prix) moved McLaren up to first in the [World Constructors' Championship](3437393.md). This was the first time since [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Formula_One_World_Championship) that McLaren had led the WCC.[^cite_note-193] In Abu Dhabi, Norris finished in first to win McLaren's first World Constructors' Championship since 1998.[^cite_note-194]&#x20;

Piastri driving an orange McLaren MCL39

An improvement on the successful MCL38, the MCL39, took consistent podiums and won fourteen of the twenty-four races in which it competed, giving the team enough momentum to secure their tenth Constructors' Championship, and their first Drivers' Championship since [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Formula_One_World_Championship).

With McLaren winning the WCC the previous season, they were allocated less testing time in [2025](72768531.md), and were deemed the favourites to defend the Constructors' Championship.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196] The [MCL39](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL39) was the first McLaren car to be fully overseen by Rob Marshall, and it exhibited numerous technical improvements over its predecessor. The MCL39 showed an immediate advantage over the rest of the field; McLaren won fourteen out of twenty-four races in 2025 season, with Norris and Piastri both scoring seven wins each - including seven 1-2 finishes - and scored podiums in all possible races, bar three: the [Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Canadian_Grand_Prix), [Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Azerbaijan_Grand_Prix), and [Las Vegas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Las_Vegas_Grand_Prix) Grands Prix. Despite an uptick in pace from [Red Bull](1173193.md), McLaren was able to secure their tenth championship at the [Singapore Grand Prix](77743466.md), becoming the most-successful Formula One [independent constructor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One_teams) of all time to date. Amidst the team's dominance in winning the Constructors' Championship, a battle for the Drivers' Championship between Norris and Piastri began: Norris led the early stages of the championship before Piastri took the lead after capitalising on Norris' mistake in qualifying for the [Saudi Arabian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Saudi_Arabian_Grand_Prix). As the season drew to a close, Piastri experienced a downturn in form, which also coincided with two crashes at the Azerbaijan and [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_S%C3%A3o_Paulo_Grand_Prix) Grands Prix. Norris and Red Bull's [Max Verstappen](41758713.md) took advantage of this, with the former in particular recovering from a gap as high as 34 points after the [Dutch Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Dutch_Grand_Prix) to eventually retake the lead of the Drivers' Championship by one point by winning the [Mexico City Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Mexico_City_Grand_Prix). Norris and Piastri were both disqualified from the [Las Vegas Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Las_Vegas_Grand_Prix) due to a plank wear infringement, marking the first time both McLaren drivers were disqualified in one Grand Prix.[^cite_note-197] The result allowed Verstappen to be level on points with Piastri. Finishing in fourth with both Verstappen and Piastri on the podium, Norris was unable to secure the Drivers' Championship at the [Qatar Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Qatar_Grand_Prix), bringing the championship fight to the final race of the season, the [Abu Dhabi Grand Prix](77743503.md). Piastri and Norris finished the Abu Dhabi Grand Prix in second and third, respectively. The third place finish allowed Norris to win his first Drivers' Championship and McLaren's first Drivers' Championship since 2008. This also marked McLaren's first double championship winning season since 1998.[^cite_note-198]&#x20;

#### 2026: New regulations and World Championship defence

[Major changes](71681684.md#Regulation_changes) to the [regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One_regulations) were observed for [2026](71681684.md), which marked Norris' first season as defending Drivers' Champion, with Piastri continuing to race alongside him. McLaren also entered the season as defending Constructors' Champions, with their [MCL40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL40) debuting at a pre-season shakedown at [Circuit de Barcelona-Catalunya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_de_Barcelona-Catalunya). McLaren experienced numerous struggles over the first part of the season. Their car, while a contender for the top five positions, was indeed off the pace of Mercedes and [Ferrari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scuderia_Ferrari).[^cite_note-199] At the [Australian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Australian_Grand_Prix), Norris finished fifth and Piastri crashed his MCL40 heavily before the race began.[^cite_note-200] McLaren failed to start the [Chinese Grand Prix](80284337.md), their 1000th entry,[^cite_note-201] after facing [power unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes_AMG_High_Performance_Powertrains) issues; marking the first time this has happened since the controversial [2005 United States Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_United_States_Grand_Prix).[^cite_note-202] McLaren returned to the podium at the [Japanese Grand Prix](80284355.md), by then becoming the second-best car behind the [Mercedes W17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes_W17). A pointless weekend ensued at the [Canadian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Canadian_Grand_Prix) with Norris retiring, again due to power unit issues, and Piastri finishing outside the points. As 2026 marked the 60th anniversary of the running of the [1966 Monaco Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Monaco_Grand_Prix), where the team made its debut, the team opted to celebrate their 1000th entry at the following race, the [2026 Monaco Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Monaco_Grand_Prix).[^cite_note-203] At the [Spanish Grand Prix](79774929.md), Norris secured his third pole position of the season.[^cite_note-204] During the race, Norris led comfortably ahead of Kimi Antonelli and Max Verstappen until Lance Stroll stopped on lap 13. The virtual safety car was called on lap 14 just seconds after Norris had passed the pit entry. Norris pitted on the following lap after the virtual safety car ended and came out in fifth, eventually finishing the race in third behind Antonelli and Verstappen.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206]&#x20;

## Racing history: Other series

Outside of the Formula One World Championship, McLaren's greatest initial success was in Can-Am, which they dominated from 1967 to 1971. Further American triumph followed, with Indianapolis 500 wins in McLaren cars for [Mark Donohue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Donohue) in [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Indianapolis_500) and [Johnny Rutherford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnny_Rutherford) in [1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Indianapolis_500) and [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Indianapolis_500). After initially returning to the [Indianapolis 500](159018.md) in [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Indianapolis_500) as a backer of [Andretti Autosport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andretti_Autosport) to run [Fernando Alonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Alonso) and then in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indianapolis_500) as an independent entry, McLaren announced in August 2019 that they would run in conjunction with Arrow Schmidt Peterson Motorsports starting in [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_IndyCar_Series) to run the full [IndyCar Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IndyCar_Series), the combined entry being named [Arrow McLaren SP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_McLaren_SP).[^cite_note-207] Initially having no ownership interest in the team, McLaren would purchase 75% of the operation in 2021.[^cite_note-208]&#x20;

Track-modified McLaren [road cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_Automotive) have seen success in [sports car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_car_racing) and [endurance racing](699018.md), with a [modified version](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_F1_GTR) of the [McLaren F1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_F1) road car taking victory at the [1995 24 Hours of Le Mans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_24_Hours_of_Le_Mans); the team is set to rejoin the top class of the [FIA World Endurance Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIA_World_Endurance_Championship) in 2027, with McLaren Racing CEO [Zak Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zak_Brown)'s team [United Autosports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Autosports) partnering to race a [hypercar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LMDh) to fit with the WEC's LMDh formula regulations. McLaren entered the electric [off-road racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Off-road_racing) series [Extreme E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_E) from [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Extreme_E_Championship) to [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Extreme_E_Championship), and also entered [Formula E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_E) from the [2022–23 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_Formula_E_World_Championship) to the [2024–25 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%9325_Formula_E_World_Championship).&#x20;

### Can-Am

Orange McLaren M1A sports car

The McLaren M1A [sports car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_car_racing) of 1964 was the team's first self-designed car. The 'B' version raced in [Can-Am](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Can-Am) in the [1966 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Can-Am_season).

McLaren's first racing car designed and built "from the rubber up" by Bruce McLaren Motor Racing was the M1. Bruce McLaren won races with the small-block Oldsmobile-powered car. The car was raced in North America and Europe in 1964 in various A sports and United States Road Racing Championship events. In 1965 the team car was the M1A prototype from which the production Elva M1As were based. In late 1965, the M1B (also known as Mk2) was the team car for the North American races at the end of the year. For the Can-Am Series, which started in 1966, McLaren created the M3 which Bruce and [Chris Amon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Amon) drove – customer cars also appeared in several races in the [1966 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Can-Am_season). With the M3, they led two races but scored no wins, and the inaugural title was taken by John Surtees in a Lola T70. The following year, Robin Herd purpose-designed the [Chevrolet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevrolet) V8-powered [M6A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_M6A), delays with the Formula One programme allowing the team to spend extra resources on developing the Can-Am car which was the first to be painted in McLaren orange. With Denny Hulme now partnering Bruce, they won five of six races and Bruce won the championship, setting the pattern for the next four years. In the [1968 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Can-Am_season), they used a new car, the M8, to win four races; non-works McLarens took the other two, but this time Hulme was victorious overall. In the [1969 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Can-Am_season), McLaren domination became total as they won all 11 races with the M8B; Hulme won five, and Bruce won six and the Drivers' Championship.[^cite_note-clockworkorange-28] From 1969 onwards, McLaren M12 – the customer "variant" of the M8 – was driven by several entrants, including a version modified by Jim Hall of Chaparral fame. McLaren's success in Can-Am brought with it financial rewards, both prize money and money from selling cars to other teams, that helped to support the team and fund the nascent and relatively poor-paying Formula One programme.[^cite_note-clockworkorange-28][^cite_note-209]&#x20;

Orange McLaren M8D Can-Am car

Bruce McLaren was killed testing a McLaren M8D at [Goodwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodwood_Circuit) in 1970.

When Bruce was killed testing the [1970 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_Can-Am_season)'s M8D, he was at first replaced by [Dan Gurney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Gurney), then later by [Peter Gethin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Gethin). They won two and one races, respectively, while Hulme won six on the way to the championship. Private teams competing in the 1970 Can-Am series included older M3Bs as well as the M12 – the customer version of the team's M8B. In the [1971 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Can-Am_season), the team held off the challenge of 1969 world champion [Jackie Stewart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Stewart) in the [Lola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lola_Cars) T260, winning eight races, with Peter Revson taking the title. Hulme also won three Can-Am races in the [1972 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Can-Am_season), but the [McLaren M20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_M20) was defeated by the [Porsche 917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porsche_917)/10s of [Mark Donohue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Donohue) and [George Follmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Follmer). Faced by the greater resources of Porsche, McLaren decided to abandon Can-Am at the end of 1972 and focus solely on [open-wheel racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_wheel_car).[^cite_note-clockworkorange-28] When the original Can-Am series ceased at the end of the [1974 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Can-Am_season), McLaren was by far the most successful constructor with 43 wins.[^cite_note-210]&#x20;

### American open-wheel racing

#### USAC (1970–1979)

McLaren M16C in orange livery

The McLaren M16C was driven by Peter Revson in the [1972 Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Indianapolis_500).

McLaren first contested the [United States Auto Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Auto_Club)'s (USAC) Indianapolis 500 race in [1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_Indianapolis_500), encouraged by their tyre supplier [Goodyear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodyear_Tire_and_Rubber_Company), which wanted to break competitor [Firestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firestone_Tire_and_Rubber_Company)'s stranglehold on the event. With the M15 car, Bruce, Chris Amon, and Denny Hulme entered, but after Amon withdrew and Hulme was severely burned on the hands in an incident in practice, Peter Revson and [Carl Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Williams_\(driver\)) took their places in the race to retire and finish seventh, respectively. The team also contested some of the more prestigious races in the USAC championship that year, as they would do in subsequent years.[^cite_note-211] For 1971 they had a new car, the M16, which driver Mark Donohue said: "...obsoleted every other car on track..." At that year's Indianapolis 500, Revson qualified on pole and finished second, whilst in [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Indianapolis_500), Donohue won in [privateer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privateer_\(motorsport\)) [Team Penske](2321219.md)'s M16B.[^cite_note-212] The [1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Indianapolis_500) event had [Johnny Rutherford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnny_Rutherford) join the team; he qualified on pole, but finished ninth, Revson crashed out.[^cite_note-213] McLaren won their first Indianapolis 500 in [1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Indianapolis_500) with Rutherford. The McLaren and Rutherford combination was second in [1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_Indianapolis_500) and won again in [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Indianapolis_500).[^cite_note-214] Developments of the M16 had been used throughout this period until the new M24 car was introduced in 1977. The team did not reproduce their recent success at Indianapolis in [1977](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Indianapolis_500), [1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Indianapolis_500), or [1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Indianapolis_500), and although they continued to win other USAC races, by the end of 1979, they decided to end their involvement to concentrate on their Formula One programme.[^cite_note-215]&#x20;

Orange-liveried IndyCar on track

The car backed by McLaren at the [2017 Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Indianapolis_500), which was driven by Fernando Alonso

#### IndyCar Series

##### 2017 and 2019 Indianapolis 500

On 12 April 2017, McLaren revealed they would participate in the [2017 Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Indianapolis_500) with their current Formula 1 driver Fernando Alonso at the wheel of a Honda-powered McLaren-branded [Andretti Autosport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andretti_Autosport) IndyCar.[^cite_note-216] In qualifying, Alonso secured a second-row start from fifth.[^cite_note-217] During the race Alonso led 27 laps in his first Indy 500 start. With 21 laps remaining Alonso was running seventh when his Honda engine failed.[^cite_note-218] He was classified 24th. After his retirement he received a standing ovation from the grandstands.[^cite_note-219] Alonso was praised for his strong debut.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221]&#x20;

On 10 November 2018, McLaren announced that they would participate in the [2019 Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indianapolis_500) with Fernando Alonso and using [Chevrolet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevrolet) engines.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] However, after mechanical difficulties and a severe crash in practice, the team failed to qualify for the race.[^cite_note-224]&#x20;

| Race victories | 12 |
| [Pole positions](630713.md) | 11 |

In August 2019, it was announced McLaren would contest the championship full-time in [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_IndyCar_Series), collaborating with [Arrow Schmidt Peterson Motorsports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_Schmidt_Peterson_Motorsports)[^cite_note-225] to form Arrow McLaren SP.&#x20;

Zak Brown stated in an interview with [Leigh Diffey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leigh_Diffey) that McLaren joining the IndyCar Series full time was spurred by two different objectives. The first was to market the McLaren brand and some of the McLaren Formula One team's prominent American based sponsors in a primarily North America centric racing series, as Formula One only had three races in North America in [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Formula_One_World_Championship) and only one of those races was in the United States. The second was to branch McLaren's engineering expertise into a racing series that the other Formula One teams were not involved in, as Brown thought McLaren would stand out more amongst its competitors in IndyCar than it would in other racing series. Brown also stated that McLaren chose to partner with Schmidt Peterson Motorsports because their previous efforts fielding their team with assistance from Andretti Autosport and Carlin exclusively for the Indianapolis 500 had not been successful and that the purchase of the IndyCar Series by [Penske Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Penske) gave McLaren more confidence in the long term viability and stability of the series compared to the previous ownership under [Tony George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_George).[^cite_note-226]&#x20;

In August 2021, it was announced that McLaren Racing will acquire a majority stake in the IndyCar Team. The transaction closed by the end of the year and saw McLaren Racing take a 75% share of the team. Financial terms of the deal were not disclosed.[^cite_note-227]&#x20;

For the [2022 IndyCar Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_IndyCar_Series), the team's first under McLaren ownership, both O'Ward and Rosenqvist would return to the team as full time entries. The No. 6 car would again return on a part-time basis for the [GMR Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GMR_Grand_Prix) and the [2022 Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Indianapolis_500) driven by Juan Pablo Montoya.[^cite_note-228] O'Ward and Rosenqvist would finish second and fourth in the Indianapolis 500 respectively, the team's best finish at Indianapolis to date. The team announced they had signed [Alexander Rossi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Rossi) to drive a third full time car from 2023 and beyond.[^cite_note-Indycar_Group-229]&#x20;

For [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_IndyCar_Series), the team announced they had signed [Alexander Rossi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Rossi) to drive a third full time car.[^cite_note-Indycar_Group-229] Team president Taylor Kiel also left; his duties were redistributed between [Brian Barnhart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Barnhart), who joins the team with Rossi from Andretti Autosport as General Manager, and [Gavin Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gavin_Ward_\(engineer\)).[^cite_note-230] In conjunction with McLaren's 60th-anniversary celebration, the team raced a special livery for the [2023 Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Indianapolis_500) to celebrate McLaren's [Triple Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Crown_of_Motorsport) achievement. The liveries of the Nos. 7, 6 and 5 were painted as the [McLaren M16C/D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_M16) that won the [1974 Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Indianapolis_500), the [McLaren MP4/2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MP4/2) that won the [1984 Monaco Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Monaco_Grand_Prix), and the [McLaren F1 GTR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_F1_GTR) that won the [1995 24 Hours of Le Mans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_24_Hours_of_Le_Mans) respectively to honour the three winning McLaren cars that forms the Triple Crown.[^cite_note-Triple_Crown_Liveries_Revealed-231][^cite_note-232]&#x20;

For 2024, Rosenqvist moved to [Meyer Shank Racing](30697656.md) and was replaced by [David Malukas](58540280.md), who moved from [Dale Coyne Racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Coyne_Racing).[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234] However, after injuring his left wrist early in the year and missing out on the start of the season, Malukas was released from the team replaced by [Théo Pourchaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9o_Pourchaire).[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236]&#x20;

### Electric racing

[Neom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neom) was McLaren's title partner into their endeavour to [electric motorsport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_motorsport) as NEOM McLaren Electric Racing.[^cite_note-McLarenNEOM-237]&#x20;

#### Extreme E (2022–2024)

In June 2021, McLaren announced it would enter [Extreme E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_E) in the [2022 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Extreme_E_Championship) using existing personnel from outside the Formula One programme[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] with [Tanner Foust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanner_Foust) and [Emma Gilmour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emma_Gilmour) (who becomes the first woman factory driver for McLaren) as drivers for the team.[^cite_note-240] Entering as McLaren XE, the team was rebranded for their second race as NEOM McLaren Extreme E Team for sponsorship reasons.[^cite_note-McLarenNEOM-237] The team won its first podium by finishing second in the [Energy X-Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Energy_X-Prix). In the process, Gilmour became the first woman podium winner for McLaren.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242] The team finished in fifth place in the Team's Championship standings.&#x20;

McLaren retained Foust and Gilmour for the [2023 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Extreme_E_Championship).[^cite_note-243] The team won its second podium by finishing second in Round 4 at the [Hydro X-Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Hydro_X-Prix).[^cite_note-244] In Round 7 at the Island X-Prix II, Gilmour suffered a fractured rib and a concussion following a crash during the first free practice session and was ruled out for the rest of the weekend. She was replaced by Extreme E's championship reserve driver [Tamara Molinaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamara_Molinaro) for Rounds 7 and 8.[^cite_note-245] McLaren withdrew from Round 8 due to the spare car being too heavily damaged in Round 7's redemption race after Molinaro collided with [JBXE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JBXE)'s [Hedda Hosås](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hedda_Hos%C3%A5s) on the run down to the first jump at the start of the race and rolled the car. This is the first instance of a team withdrawing from a race in Extreme E.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] For the final two rounds of the season, McLaren announced that Gilmour was still recovering from her injuries and was replaced by Hosås.[^cite_note-248] McLaren finished in eighth place in the Team's Championship standings. At the end of the season, McLaren announced that Foust and Gilmour will leave the team.[^cite_note-249]&#x20;

For the [2024 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Extreme_E_Championship), McLaren announced a new driver pairing of [Cristina Gutiérrez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristina_Guti%C3%A9rrez) and [Mattias Ekström](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mattias_Ekstr%C3%B6m), who moved from [X44](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_X44) and [Sainz XE Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Sainz_Sr.#Sainz_XE_Team) respectively.[^cite_note-250] The team won its first podium of the season by finishing second in Round 1 at the [Desert X-Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Desert_X-Prix).[^cite_note-251] On 6 September, a week before the scheduled Island X-Prix, Extreme E announced that the rounds in Sardinia and Phoenix were cancelled as the series transition to [Extreme H](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_H) for 2025.[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253]&#x20;

#### Formula E (2023–2025)

In December 2020, Zak Brown announced McLaren's interest in entering [Formula E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_E) once the company's battery supplier contract has expired.[^cite_note-254] In January the following year, McLaren signed an option to enter the championship for 2022.[^cite_note-255]&#x20;

In May 2022, McLaren announced the acquisition of the [Mercedes-EQ Formula E Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes-EQ_Formula_E_Team) and debuted in the [2022–23 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_Formula_E_World_Championship) as NEOM McLaren Formula E Team using [Nissan's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nismo) EV powertrain.[^cite_note-\:1-256][^cite_note-257] [René Rast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Rast), who last raced in the [2020–21 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%9321_Formula_E_World_Championship) with [Audi Sport ABT Schaeffler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abt_Sportsline), and [Jake Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Hughes) were signed as drivers for the team.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] McLaren made its ePrix debut at the [2023 Mexico City ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Mexico_City_ePrix). Hughes qualified in third and finished the race in fifth place whereas Rast qualified in fifteenth but retired from the race on lap 40 after colliding with [Mahindra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahindra_Racing)'s [Oliver Rowland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Rowland).[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262] McLaren achieved several milestones at the [Diriyah ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Diriyah_ePrix). In the [first round](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Diriyah_ePrix#Race_one), Hughes qualified at second place (missing out on pole position by 0.060s) but finished in eighth whereas Rast qualified and finished in fifth place while scoring McLaren's first fastest lap in Formula E.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264] In the [second round](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Diriyah_ePrix#Race_two), Hughes secured McLaren's maiden pole position in Formula E and Rast qualified in third place.[^cite_note-265] Hughes finished the race in fifth and Rast finished in third place, giving McLaren their maiden podium finish in Formula E.[^cite_note-266] Hughes qualified in second at the [Monaco ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Monaco_ePrix) but was later promoted to pole position after [Nissan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nismo#Formula_E)'s [Sacha Fenestraz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacha_Fenestraz) was stripped of his pole position for exceeding his power limit in his final run.[^cite_note-267] Hughes finished the race in fifth place whereas Rast finished in seventeenth after suffering from multiple collisions.[^cite_note-268] McLaren finished the season in eighth in the Teams' Championship.&#x20;

In August 2023, McLaren announced that Hughes had re-signed with the team whereas Rast will leave the team and was replaced by [Sam Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Bird) for the [2023–24 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023%E2%80%9324_Formula_E_World_Championship).[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] Bird won McLaren's maiden electric race victory at the [São Paulo ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_S%C3%A3o_Paulo_ePrix_\(March\)).[^cite_note-271] At the [Monaco ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Monaco_ePrix), Bird suffered a hand injury during the first free practice session and was replaced by reserve and development driver [Taylor Barnard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_Barnard).[^cite_note-272] Barnard qualified in 22nd and finished his debut race in 14th while becoming the [youngest driver and the first teenager to start an ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Formula_E_driver_records#Youngest_drivers_to_start_a_race) at 19 years and 331 days.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] Barnard continued to deputise for Bird at the [Berlin ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Berlin_ePrix), where he finished Race 1 in tenth to become [youngest point scorer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Formula_E_driver_records#Youngest_drivers_to_score_points).[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] Hughes left McLaren to join [Maserati MSG Racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maserati_MSG_Racing) in mid-2024[^cite_note-278] and was replaced by Barnard, who was promoted to compete full-time in the [2024–25 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%9325_Formula_E_World_Championship).[^cite_note-279]&#x20;

At the season opening [São Paulo ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_S%C3%A3o_Paulo_ePrix_\(December\)), Barnard won his maiden podium by finishing in third while also becoming the [youngest podium finisher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Formula_E_driver_records#Youngest_drivers_to_score_a_podium_finish) at the age of 20 years and 189 days whereas Bird finished in fourth.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281] Barnard followed up with another podium finish by coming in third at [Race 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Jeddah_ePrix#Race_1) of the [Jeddah ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Jeddah_ePrix).[^cite_note-282] At [Race 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Jeddah_ePrix#Race_2), he scored his first pole position, becoming the [youngest polesitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Formula_E_driver_records#Youngest_polesitters) at the age of 20 years 259 days[^cite_note-283] but failed to convert it to a win, finishing the race in second.[^cite_note-284] In April 2025, McLaren announced that they will exit Formula E at the end of the 2024–25 season to focus on development for the World Endurance Championship.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] It was later reported that the team will be closed down after McLaren failed to find a buyer and the team entry will be returned to Formula E.[^cite_note-287] McLaren finished their final Formula E race at [Race 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_London_ePrix#Race_2) of the [London ePrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_London_ePrix) with a double DNF. McLaren ended the season in sixth in the Teams' Championship whereas Barnard ended his debut season in fourth in the Drivers' Championship, the highest for a McLaren driver.&#x20;

In 2026, McLaren was fined €400,000 by the FIA for a “minor overspending” breach of the cost cap regulations.[^cite_note-288]&#x20;

### Endurance racing

| --- | --- |
| Base | [McLaren Technology Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_Technology_Centre) [Woking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woking), Surrey, England |
| Team principal(s) | James Barclay |
| Noted drivers | [Denmark](76972.md) [Mikkel Jensen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikkel_Jensen_\(racing_driver\)) [Belgium](3343.md) [Laurens Vanthoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurens_Vanthoor) |

In 2021, McLaren reviewed the [LMH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Mans_Hypercar)/[LMDh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LMDh) and GTP regulations for a possible entry into the [FIA World Endurance Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIA_World_Endurance_Championship) (WEC) Hypercar and [IMSA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Motor_Sports_Association) [WeatherTech SportsCar Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_SportsCar_Championship) GTP classes respectively in the future.[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291] On 19 June 2024, CEO Zak Brown announced that McLaren is planning a Hypercar class entry with the LMDh regulations by the 2027 season.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] On 10 April 2025, McLaren announced that they would enter WEC in the Hypercar class utilising the LMDh formula regulations starting from the [2027 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2027_FIA_World_Endurance_Championship) onwards.[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295] McLaren will collaborate with Brown's [United Autosports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Autosports) and enter the season as McLaren United AS with James Barclay as team principal. McLaren United will also partner with [Dallara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallara) as the chassis partner. On 24 April 2026, the new car was named as the MCL-HY.[^cite_note-296]&#x20;

### Customer racing

McLaren F1 GTR racing at Paul Ricard

The [McLaren F1 GTR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_F1_GTR), competing during the [1995 BPR Global GT Series season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_BPR_Global_GT_Series_season)

Besides the cars raced by the works team, a variety of McLaren racing cars have also been used by customer teams. In their formative years, McLaren built Formula Two,[^cite_note-nye92-297] [hillclimbing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillclimbing),[^cite_note-298] [Formula 5000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_5000)[^cite_note-nyeappendix2-299] and sports racing cars[^cite_note-nye76-300] that were sold to customers. Lacking the capacity to build the desired numbers, [Trojan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_\(racing_team\)) was [subcontracted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subcontractor) to construct some of them.[^cite_note-nye92-297][^cite_note-nyeappendix2-299][^cite_note-nye76-300] In Can-Am, Trojan built customer versions of the M6 and M8 cars and ex-works cars were sold to privateers when new models arrived; half of the field was McLarens at some races. Author [Mark Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Hughes_\(journalist\)) says, "over 220" McLarens were built by Trojan.[^cite_note-clockworkorange-28] In USAC competition and Formula One, too, many teams used McLarens during the late 1960s and 1970s.[^cite_note-301] A 1972 M8F was rebuilt as the [C8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_C8) for use in [Group C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_C) racing in 1982, but had little success.[^cite_note-302]&#x20;

In the mid-1990s, McLaren Racing's sister company, [McLaren Cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_Automotive) (now McLaren Automotive) built a racing version of their [F1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_F1) road car, the [F1 GTR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_F1_GTR) which won the [1995 24 Hours of Le Mans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_24_Hours_of_Le_Mans) and the 1995 and 1996 [BPR Global GT Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BPR_Global_GT_Series).[^cite_note-303] In 2011, a [GT3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_GT3) version of the [MP4-12C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MP4-12C) road car was developed in partnership with CRS Racing, making its competitive debut at the [VLN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_VLN_Series) and [ADAC GT Masters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_ADAC_GT_Masters) in 2012.[^cite_note-304] The MP4-12C was succeeded by the [McLaren 650S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_650S) and then the [McLaren 720S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_720S) for GT3 racing, while a GT4 version of the McLaren 570S was also developed as well.&#x20;

In 2022, McLaren Automotive announced a new GT4 model based on the [McLaren Artura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_Artura),[^cite_note-305] along with an unrestricted version named the Artura Trophy, which is to be used in McLaren's planned one-make series.[^cite_note-306]&#x20;

In October 2023, McLaren Automotive announced its intent to participate in the [2024 24 Hours of Le Mans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_24_Hours_of_Le_Mans) under the new [LMGT3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_GT3) category through CEO Zak Brown's [United Autosports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Autosports) as its customer team.[^cite_note-307] McLaren Automotive also announced that it will enter the [2024 IMSA SportsCar Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_IMSA_SportsCar_Championship) under the [GT Daytona Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_GT3) (GTD Pro) category through Pfaff Motorsports as its customer team.[^cite_note-308] Both United Autosports and Pfaff Motorsports will be using the [McLaren 720S GT3 Evo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_720S#720S_GT3_Evo). In the following month, McLaren Automotive confirmed its participation in the [2024 FIA World Endurance Championship season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_FIA_World_Endurance_Championship) in the LMGT3 category in collaboration with United Autosports.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310]&#x20;

## Characteristics

McLaren Racing is majority owned by the [McLaren Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_Group), having sold 15% of the team to American investors MSP Sports Capital in 2020, rising to a 33% stake in 2022.[^cite_note-RacingSale-311] The team had previously been wholly owned by the Group since its inception.&#x20;

### Ownership and management

Ron Dennis in a suit

[Ron Dennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Dennis) (pictured at the [2000 Monaco Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Monaco_Grand_Prix)) was team principal from 1980 to 2009 and the chairman of the [McLaren Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_Group) until 2017.

After Bruce McLaren died in a testing accident in 1970, [Teddy Mayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teddy_Mayer) took over the team. In 1981, McLaren merged with Ron Dennis' Project Four Racing; Dennis took over as team principal and shortly after organised a buyout of the original McLaren shareholders to take full control of the team. Dennis offered [Mansour Ojjeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansour_Ojjeh) the chance to purchase 50% of the team in 1983, with McLaren becoming a joint venture with Ojjeh's [TAG Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TAG_Group). In 2000, after supplying engines to the team through its [Mercedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes-Benz_High_Performance_Engines) subsidiary for 5 years, [DaimlerChrysler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DaimlerChrysler) (now [Daimler AG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daimler_AG)) exercised an option to buy 40% of the TAG McLaren Group.[^cite_note-312] Dennis and Ojjeh each retained a 30% share,[^cite_note-313] and each sold half of their stake to the [Mumtalakat Holding Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mumtalakat_Holding_Company) (the [sovereign wealth fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_wealth_fund) of the [Kingdom of Bahrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bahrain)) in 2007.[^cite_note-314] Although Daimler were reportedly considering acquiring the remaining 60% from Dennis and Ojjeh, they instead bought [Brawn GP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brawn_GP) (renaming it [Mercedes GP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes_GP)) in November 2009;[^cite_note-mercedesbrawn-315] their McLaren shares were sold back to Mumtalakat, Dennis, and Ojjeh in 2010.[^cite_note-PwM-316]&#x20;

Dennis stepped down as both CEO and team principal of McLaren in 2009, handing both roles over to Martin Whitmarsh.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] However, following the uncompetitive 2013 season, Dennis retook the role in January 2014;[^cite_note-BBCSport-319] Whitmarsh formally left the team later that year.[^cite_note-320] Dennis sought to take a controlling interest in the company, but his relationship with Ojjeh had deteriorated, perhaps as early as 2013.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-Dennis02-322] In 2016, Dennis was forced out of his role as CEO by Ojjeh.[^cite_note-bbcdennis-323] He sold his remaining shares in the company the next year.[^cite_note-autosportdennis-324]&#x20;

After Dennis' 2014 return, he had abolished the position of team principal at McLaren, saying it was an 'outdated' position.[^cite_note-325] [Éric Boullier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89ric_Boullier) was instead named racing director in January 2014, becoming responsible for the F1 team.[^cite_note-326] After Dennis' exit, [Zak Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zak_Brown) was chosen for the post of Group executive director, with the positions of Group CEO and Racing CEO both being left vacant.[^cite_note-327] While his position was formally within the wider McLaren Group, it was understood that his role would focus only on the F1 team. The increasing awareness of the mediocrity of the car prompted a reshuffle in 2018: Brown was appointed McLaren Racing CEO in April, and when Boullier resigned in July, his position was divided between [Gil de Ferran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gil_de_Ferran) as sporting director and [Andrea Stella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrea_Stella_\(engineer\)) as performance director.[^cite_note-328] In May 2019, [Andreas Seidl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Seidl) was appointed as the new team principal.[^cite_note-329] In December 2022, Seidl left McLaren to join [Sauber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauber_Motorsport) as CEO with Stella promoted to team principal.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331]&#x20;

Since 2004 the team has been based at the McLaren Technology Centre in Woking, England.[^cite_note-332] Facilities there include a wind tunnel and a driving simulator which is said to be the most sophisticated in the sport.[^cite_note-333] The team also created the [McLaren Driver Development Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_Driver_Development_Programme).[^cite_note-334]&#x20;

### Politics

McLaren has had an uneasy relationship with Formula One's governing body, the FIA, and its predecessor FISA, as well as with the commercial rights holders of the sport. McLaren was involved, along with the other teams of the [Formula One Constructors Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One_Constructors_Association) (FOCA), in a dispute with FISA and Alfa Romeo, Renault, and Ferrari over control of the sport in the early 1980s. During this dispute, known as the [FISA-FOCA war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FISA-FOCA_war), [a breakaway series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FISA%E2%80%93FOCA_war#Proposed_World_Professional_Drivers_Championship_1981_Events) was threatened, FISA refused to sanction [one race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_South_African_Grand_Prix), and FOCA [boycotted another](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_San_Marino_Grand_Prix). It was eventually resolved by a revenue-sharing deal called the [Concorde Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concorde_Agreement).[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337]&#x20;

Subsequent Concorde Agreements were signed in 1987 and 1992, but in 1996, McLaren was again one of the teams which disputed the terms of a new agreement, this time with former FOCA president [Bernie Ecclestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Ecclestone)'s [Formula One Promotions and Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One_Promotions_and_Administration) organisation; a new 10-year agreement was eventually signed in 1998.[^cite_note-338] Similar arguments restarted in the mid-2000s, with McLaren and their part-owner Mercedes again threatening to start a rival series, before another Concorde Agreement was signed in 2009.[^cite_note-339] In 2007, McLaren were involved in an [espionage controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Formula_One_espionage_controversy) after their chief designer [Mike Coughlan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Coughlan) obtained confidential technical information from Ferrari. McLaren was excluded from the Constructors' Championship for one year, and the team was fined US\$100 million.[^cite_note-mclarenban-106][^cite_note-wmsc-340] Although the terms of the most recent agreements, in 2013 and 2021, have been extensively negotiated on, McLaren have not taken as openly hostile a stance as in the past.&#x20;

During Brown's tenure as CEO, McLaren has been critical of collaboration between teams, particularly [Red Bull GmbH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Bull_GmbH)'s ownership of two teams.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342]&#x20;

### Facilities

In 2026, McLaren Racing expanded its Indianapolis base with the opening of the McLaren Racing Center, a newly renovated \$30 million facility that increased the team's footprint from approximately 33,000 sq ft to around 86,000 sq ft to support its IndyCar operations.[^cite_note-\:3-343] The space includes 12 dedicated car bays, indoor space for team haulers, a pit-stop practice area, office space, and an integrated gym and recovery facility.[^cite_note-\:3-343]&#x20;

## Sponsorship, livery and naming

### Title sponsors

Red-and-white Marlboro McLaren cars on display

McLaren's Formula One team was sponsored for 23 years by [Philip Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Morris_International)'s [Marlboro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marlboro_\(cigarette\)) cigarette brand.

In [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Formula_One_season), the [Royal Automobile Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Automobile_Club) and the FIA relaxed the rules regarding commercial sponsorship of Formula One cars. In [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Formula_One_season), [Yardley London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yardley_London), a cosmetics company, became McLaren's first title sponsor and the team raced as Yardley Team McLaren.[^cite_note-344][^cite_note-tremayne246-345] As a result, the livery was changed to a predominantly white one to reflect the sponsor's colours.[^cite_note-346] In [1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Formula_One_season), [Philip Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Morris_International) was signed as title sponsor through their [Marlboro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marlboro) cigarette brand although one car continued to run-ostensibly by a separate team-with Yardley livery for the year.[^cite_note-tremayne246-345] Marlboro's red-and-white branding lasted until [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Formula_One_World_Championship), during which time the team went by the many variants of the Marlboro McLaren name. This partnership was the then longest-running Formula One sponsorship which has since been surpassed by [Hugo Boss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Boss)' sponsorship of the team, which ran from [1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Formula_One_World_Championship) to [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Formula_One_World_Championship). The McLaren–Marlboro title sponsorship deal is still the longest-running in Formula One.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-mclarenseasons-350]&#x20;

In [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Formula_One_World_Championship), Philip Morris moved its Marlboro sponsorship to Ferrari and was replaced by [Reemtsma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reemtsma)'s [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_\(cigarette\)) cigarette brand, with the team racing as West McLaren Mercedes.[^cite_note-351] As a result, McLaren adopted a silver and black livery. By mid-2005, a [European Union directive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directive_\(European_Union\)) banned [tobacco advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco_advertising) in sport, which forced McLaren to end its association with West.[^cite_note-352] In [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Formula_One_World_Championship), the team competed without a title sponsor, entering under the name Team McLaren Mercedes. McLaren altered their livery to introduce red into the design, and changed the silver to chrome. In [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Formula_One_World_Championship), McLaren signed a seven-year contract with telecommunications company [Vodafone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vodafone) and competed as Vodafone McLaren Mercedes.[^cite_note-353] The arrangement was due to last until [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Formula_One_World_Championship), although the team announced at the [2013 Australian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Australian_Grand_Prix) that their partnership would conclude at the end of the 2013 season.[^cite_note-354] Despite explaining the decision to conclude the sponsorship as being a result of Vodafone's desire to reconsider its commercial opportunities, *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* reported that the decision to run the [2012 Bahrain Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Bahrain_Grand_Prix) despite an [ongoing civil uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Bahraini_uprising) and [protests against the race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Bahrain_Grand_Prix_protests), and Vodafone's inability to remove their logos from the McLaren cars during the race as a factor in the decision to terminate the sponsorship.[^cite_note-355] [Diageo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diageo)-owned whisky brand Johnnie Walker, an associate sponsor since 2005, offered to take over as title sponsor at the end of 2013, but their offer of £43 million was turned down by Ron Dennis, who believed it to be "too small".[^cite_note-Telegraph.co.uk-356]&#x20;

In 2015, McLaren was still without a title sponsor and was set to lose a further £20 million in sponsorship in 2016.[^cite_note-Telegraph.co.uk-356] Between [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Formula_One_World_Championship) and [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Formula_One_World_Championship), the team competed as McLaren Honda due to their engine partnership with Honda.[^cite_note-357] After splitting with Honda, the team competed as McLaren Formula 1 Team from [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Formula_One_World_Championship) onwards. CEO Zak Brown said that he will not sell the name of the team to a title sponsor to keep the team's name intact and to not rely on a single sponsor. Instead, the team will look for major sponsors, which give brands increased branding position on the car.[^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360] Brown's title sponsor position later shifted[^cite_note-361] and in August 2025, McLaren announced a deal with [Mastercard](378791.md) and will compete as McLaren Mastercard Formula 1 Team from [2026](71681684.md) onwards.[^cite_note-362] The sponsorship is valued at a reported \$100 million per season and will run to the mid-2030s.[^cite_note-363] Brown added that the livery will "stay recognisable" despite the title sponsorship.[^cite_note-364]&#x20;

### Liveries

Further information: [Formula One sponsorship liveries § McLaren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One_sponsorship_liveries#McLaren)

Orange McLaren MCL60 in standard livery

McLaren MCL60 in dark Stealth Mode livery

Since 2021, McLaren has used special liveries on their cars in collaboration with their sponsors and to commemorate certain occasions.

In the team's [debut race in 1966](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Monaco_Grand_Prix), it ran white-and-green coloured cars, which came about as a result of a deal with the makers of the film *[Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Prix_\(1966_film\))*.[^cite_note-Colours-365] Between [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Formula_One_season) and [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Formula_One_season), the team used an orange design, dubbed the papaya orange, which was also applied to cars competing in the Indianapolis 500 and Can-Am series, and was used as an interim testing livery in later years.[^cite_note-Colours-365][^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] Between 1972 and 2013, McLaren's livery colours reflected the colours of the title sponsors. Since 2017, McLaren returned to its traditional papaya orange colours, which was also used on their cars entered in other racing series such as Extreme E and Formula E.&#x20;

McLaren have a team tradition where they would run the same design the season after a championship win. The team also would not change the livery substantially during a period of sustained success.[^cite_note-Livery-368] Most recently, this was seen with the [MCL39](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL39) in [2025](72768531.md), which saw minimal changes from the [previous year's](72504121.md) [MCL38](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL38) that won McLaren's first Constructors' Championship in 26 years, and the [subsequent year's](71681684.md) [MCL40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL40) after successfully defending the Constructors' Championship and winning the team's first Drivers' Championship in 17 years.&#x20;

In [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Formula_One_World_Championship), as part of McLaren's Gulf Oil sponsorship, the team rolled out a special one-off "heritage" Gulf livery for the [Monaco Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Monaco_Grand_Prix),[^cite_note-369] which then began a trend of one-off special liveries in Formula One.[^cite_note-370] [Vuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vuse) also partnered with McLaren to race bespoke liveries designed by emerging artists for the 2021 to 2023 Abu Dhabi Grands Prix.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] McLaren's other sponsors such as OKX,[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375] [Google Chrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Chrome)[^cite_note-376] and [Jack Daniel's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Daniel%27s)[^cite_note-377] later partnered with the team to race special liveries.&#x20;

As part of McLaren's 60th-anniversary celebration during the [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Formula_One_World_Championship) season, a special livery was raced for the [Monaco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Monaco_Grand_Prix) and [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Spanish_Grand_Prix) Grands Prix to celebrate their [Triple Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Crown_of_Motorsport) achievement. The Triple Crown livery is the amalgamation of the liveries of the three winning McLaren cars that forms the Triple Crown – the papaya of the [M16C/D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_M16) that won the [1974 Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Indianapolis_500) at the rear, the white of the [MP4/2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MP4/2) that won the [1984 Monaco Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Monaco_Grand_Prix) at the middle, and the black of the [F1 GTR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_F1_GTR) that won the [1995 24 Hours of Le Mans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_24_Hours_of_Le_Mans) at the front of the [MCL60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL60).[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379] [McLaren's IndyCar Series sister team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_McLaren) also raced their version of the Triple Crown livery for the [Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Indianapolis_500), with the liveries of the Nos. 7, 6 and 5 painted as the M16C/D, MP4/2, and F1 GTR respectively.[^cite_note-Triple_Crown_Liveries_Revealed-231] At the [2026](71681684.md) [Monaco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Monaco_Grand_Prix) and [Barcelona-Catalunya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Barcelona-Catalunya_Grand_Prix) Grands Prix, McLaren raced a special livery to celebrate its 1,000th Grand Prix. The MCL40 was adorned in a bespoke metallic papaya orange and anthracite livery. The livery featured the numbers 0001 and 1000 adorning the sidepods along with details recognising the history of the team (such as the original Bruce McLaren Motor Racing logo on the base of the halo) and important milestones (such as the race dates of the first and 1,000th Grand Prix, respectively, above the numbers 0001 and 1000).[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381]&#x20;

### Car naming nomenclature

McLaren's cars were originally named with the letter M followed by a number, sometimes also followed by a letter denoting the model.[^cite_note-382] After the 1981 merger with Project Four, the cars were called "MP4/x", or since 2001 "MP4-x",[^cite_note-383] where x is the generation of the chassis (e.g. [MP4/1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MP4/1), [MP4-22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MP4-22)). "MP4" stood initially for "Marlboro Project 4",[^cite_note-384] so that the full title of the cars (McLaren MP4/x) reflected not only the historical name of the team, but also the names of the team's major sponsor and its new component part. Since the change of title sponsor in 1997, "MP4" was said to stand for "McLaren Project 4".[^cite_note-385] From 2017, following Ron Dennis' departure from the team, the naming scheme of the cars changed to "MCL" followed by a number.[^cite_note-MCL32-386] The only deviation to the numbering scheme was 2023's [MCL60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MCL60), which was named for the team's 60th anniversary.[^cite_note-mcl60-167][^cite_note-mcl601-168]&#x20;

## Race cars

As a constructor, McLaren has entered numerous race cars designed in-house into racing events, most prominently [Formula One](10854.md). As of 2025, the only exceptions to this are the [Arrow McLaren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_McLaren) [IndyCar](30446232.md) team, who run [Dallara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallara)-built chassis (initially the [IR-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallara_DW12), which was supplemented by the [IR-18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallara_DW12#IR%E2%80%9318_Universal_Aero_Kit_\(UAK-18\)_\(2018%E2%80%932026\))), McLaren's [Extreme E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_E) team, who ran the [Spark Odyssey 21](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spark_Odyssey_21), and their [Formula E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_E) operation, who ran [Formula E Gen3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_E_Gen3) with [Nissan](21240.md)'s powertrain.&#x20;

Currently, McLaren builds only [Formula One cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One_car); the most successful McLaren Formula One car is the [McLaren MP4/4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_MP4/4) of [1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Formula_One_World_Championship), also one of the most successful Formula One cars of all time. The MP4/4, driven by teammates [Ayrton Senna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayrton_Senna) and [Alain Prost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alain_Prost), took victories and pole positions in all but one race.[^cite_note-387]&#x20;

#### Drivers' champions

Eight drivers have won a total of thirteen Drivers' Championships with McLaren:[^cite_note-395]&#x20;

- [Brazil](3383.md) [Emerson Fittipaldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerson_Fittipaldi) ([1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Formula_One_season))

- [United Kingdom](31717.md) [James Hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Hunt) ([1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Formula_One_season))

- [Austria](26964606.md) [Niki Lauda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niki_Lauda) ([1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Formula_One_World_Championship))

- [France](5843419.md) [Alain Prost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alain_Prost) ([1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Formula_One_World_Championship), [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Formula_One_World_Championship), [1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Formula_One_World_Championship))

- [Brazil](3383.md) [Ayrton Senna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayrton_Senna) ([1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Formula_One_World_Championship), [1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Formula_One_World_Championship), [1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Formula_One_World_Championship))

- [Finland](10577.md) [Mika Häkkinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mika_H%C3%A4kkinen) ([1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Formula_One_World_Championship), [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Formula_One_World_Championship))

- [United Kingdom](31717.md) [Lewis Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Hamilton) ([2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Formula_One_World_Championship))

- [United Kingdom](31717.md) [Lando Norris](48978813.md) ([2025](72768531.md))

[^cite_note-25]: &#x20;Current team [Mercedes](25098794.md) first competed in [1954](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Formula_One_season)–[1955](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_Formula_One_season), but did not race again until [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Formula_One_World_Championship).<sup>[\[24\]](#cite_note-24)</sup>

[^cite_note-changes-174]: &#x20;Of these changes, James Key would be replaced as Technical Director<sup>[\[170\]](#cite_note-171)</sup> and replaced by a Technical Executive Team consisting of three new specialised Technical Director roles consisting of Peter Prodomou as Technical Director, Aerodynamics, David Sanchez (joining in January 2024 after his gardening leave ended) as Technical Director, Car concept and performance, and Neil Houdly, Technical Director Engineer and Design<sup>[\[171\]](#cite_note-172)</sup> who is a placeholder for Rob Marshall, who, like Sanchez, has been hired from a rival team but is on gardening leave until January 2024.<sup>[\[172\]](#cite_note-173)</sup>

[^cite_note-201]: &#x20;Thus becoming the second constructor after Ferrari to record entrances at 1000 Grands Prix

[^cite_note-Numbers-388]: &#x20;Individual driver numbers were not allocated at the time, as numbers differed by event.

[^cite_note-393]: &#x20;The [World Motorsport Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Motorsport_Council) disqualified McLaren from the 2007 Constructors' Championship as punishment for the [2007 Formula One espionage controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Formula_One_espionage_controversy).<sup>[\[385\]](#cite_note-bbc_espionage-389)</sup> McLaren was stripped of its Constructors' Championship points,<sup>[\[386\]](#cite_note-390)</sup> and Formula One's website states that the team scored zero points in 2007.<sup>[\[387\]](#cite_note-391)</sup> At the time McLaren was disqualified (prior to the [2007 Belgian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Belgian_Grand_Prix), with four rounds left in the season), McLaren led the Constructors' Championship with 166 points to Ferrari's 143.<sup>[\[388\]](#cite_note-392)</sup> The two McLaren drivers were not disqualified from the Drivers' Championship, and they collectively scored 218 points. However, earlier in the season, McLaren had received a 15-point deduction due to a separate controversy at the [2007 Hungarian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Hungarian_Grand_Prix). As such, in the purely hypothetical circumstance that McLaren (1) was not disqualified from the Constructors' Championship, (2) recorded the exact same results that it did in real life, (3) lost its appeal of the Hungary deduction, and (4) did not receive a second points penalty in lieu of the season disqualification, McLaren would have scored 203 points, one point behind Ferrari.

Retrieved 1 January 2026.
